Elivora (courge prostate)

https://elivorahealth.com/pages/prostate · fiche mise à jour leupdated on 27/10/2026

Analyse : angles, cible, promesses, offres, formatsAnalysis: angles, target, promises, offers, formats

En une phrase

Elivora est le concurrent le plus proche d'Uromend : courge + saw palmetto + bêta-sitostérol, angle « Arrêtez le Flomax », ≈ 1 340 pubs actives sur 2 pages (Veda Lockwood 1 000, Christopher Jay 340), presque toutes lancées en octobre 2026 (45 des 60 récoltées) : ils sont en pleine accélération. Et depuis début octobre la page Christopher Jay teste un deuxième produit, des gummies pour l'érection (ultimapeak.com).

Angles marketing (60 pubs lues)

  • « Stop taking Flomax » en tête : « I'm a urologist, and here are 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing immediately if they keep getting up to pee at night. Number one: Stop taking Flomax » (4e sur la recherche « Flomax », 18e sur « bladder support »). Le Flomax « relâche le muscle » mais « ne rétrécit pas ce qui est gonflé » ; seule « the one cold-pressed oil » le fait.
  • Le mari qui a refusé le médicament : « My husband refused Flomax / tamsulosin / finasteride / tadalafil for four years. Here's what happened to his prostate » : la même pub existe avec 4 noms de médicaments différents (7 oct 2026) pour capter chaque recherche. Narratrice : infirmière depuis 28 ans qui a « catheterized men who couldn't empty their own bladders ».
  • La chute à 3 h du matin : « Flomax had already put him face down on the hallway floor at 3 a.m., bleeding from a split eyebrow » (effet secondaire réel du Flomax : l'hypotension orthostatique, le vertige quand on se lève). Angle très concret.
  • Aliments dangereux pour la prostate : « Here are the most dangerous foods for your prostate. I do not care if you do not believe me ». Format liste.
  • La bière sacrifiée pour rien : « Too many men who poured their beer down the sink… and still got up three times a night ». Parle au mec qui a déjà fait des efforts.
  • Histoire du fondateur : « My father stopped buying pumpkin seed oil because none of it ever worked. So I flew to Styria to source the real thing » (Styrie = région d'Autriche, la courge « de référence »). Et le faux aveu « Please don't buy Elivora… We didn't warn you what it feels like to sleep through the night again ».
  • « We lied » : « We need to publicly apologize… because we lied. Yes, we're using cold-pressed virgin pumpkin seed oil, not the heat-processed powder that wrecks the fragile beta-sitosterol and delta-7 sterols » : ils attaquent les poudres (donc les gélules de poudre de courge comme… Uromend).
  • Reins / eGFR (juillet 2026, hérité d'Everly) : « I need to tell you something that might piss you off about your nephrologist ».
  • Nouveau depuis octobre : l'érection (page Christopher Jay, 19 pubs vers ultimapeak.com) : « What Happens When Your New Wife Wants Your D*ck More Than You Can Give? », « I've been lying to men about their ED for 15 years », « My Husband's Doctor Gave Him Viagra. Six Months Later He Was Dead », un « senior Pfizer researcher » qui démissionne. Langage très cru, histoires de remariage à 51 ans avec une femme plus jeune.

Cible

  • Hommes de 50 à 70 ans sous Flomax/tamsulosine, 3 à 6 levers par nuit, qui ont peur de la sonde (« thread a tube into themselves three, four, five times a day ») et de la chirurgie.
  • Leurs femmes/infirmières comme narratrices (« My husband… », « I prepped men for prostate surgery for 31 years »).
  • Depuis octobre : hommes de 47 à 58 ans divorcés ou remariés avec un problème d'érection, et leurs femmes (« For any woman whose husband is brushing off his erection problems »).

Promesses

  • Passer de 3-6 levers à 0-1 « in 12 weeks » / « four weeks after… he was getting up once a night ».
  • « Shrinks what is swollen » (rétrécit ce qui est gonflé) : promesse plus forte que « soutient ».
  • Éviter la sonde, la chirurgie, les vertiges du Flomax.
  • Gummies : érection « sans pilule bleue ».

Offres et pages de vente

  • elivorahealth.com/pages/prostate (26 pubs) : advertorial principal.
  • elivorahealth.com/pages/7-reasons-pumpkin-prostate-health (13 pubs) : page « 7 raisons », format liste.
  • ultimapeak.com/products/performance-gummies-remake (19 pubs) : le nouveau produit érection.
  • Produit : Elivora Pumpkin Seed Oil softgels (huile de Styrie, cold-pressed) + saw palmetto.

Formats

  • Quasi 100 % image + texte long (1 seule vidéo sur 60) : photo « preuve » (homme au lit, bouteille, ordonnance) + récit de 3 000 caractères signé d'un faux urologue (« Dr. Veda Lockwood », 23 ans de pratique) ou d'une infirmière.
  • Les pubs sont copiées mot pour mot d'Everly (« My husband refused Flomax for four years », « My Husband Was Getting Up 6 Times a Night », « We need to publicly apologize ») : même équipe ou même modèle.
  • Variantes par mot-clé (Flomax / tamsulosin / finasteride / tadalafil) : une pub par médicament recherché.

Ce que disent les vidéos

  • Une seule vidéo récoltée (page Christopher Jay, 14 oct 2026, histoire du « senior Pfizer researcher » pour les gummies) : transcription vide, c'est une vidéo sans voix (texte à l'écran + musique). Elivora est une marque 100 % texte long + image : tout le discours est dans les pubs ci-dessous, lisibles en entier.

Hooks, niveau de conscience, personas, ennemis, narratif (chiffres indicatifs du 17/10 ; les graphes en dessous sont recalculés à chaque relevé)

  • Hook n°1 : le récit de l'épouse (40 % des pubs) : « My husband refused Flomax / tamsulosin / finasteride / tadalafil for four years ». Puis l'autorité (« I'm a urologist… », 10 %), le faux aveu (« Please don't buy Elivora », « we lied », 10 %), la liste (« 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing », 8 %).
  • Niveau de conscience (Schwartz) : ils parlent surtout au conscient du problème (50 % : l'homme sait qu'il se lève la nuit, qu'il est sous Flomax) et à l'inconscient (35 % : « voici ce que votre urologue ne vous dit pas »). Presque jamais au « très conscient » (prix/promo : 2 %) : Elivora ne fait pas de promo dans ses pubs, elle éduque et fait peur.
  • Persona : l'homme de 50-70 ans sous médicament (63 %), puis l'homme érection (15 %, nouveau en octobre), l'épouse (12 %).
  • Ennemi : le médicament dans 95 % des pubs (Flomax en tête, tamsulosine = son générique, finastéride, tadalafil, silodosine). Pas la DHT, pas les autres marques : l'ennemi est l'ordonnance.
  • Narratif : le témoignage à la première personne (40 %), puis le récit d'expert et la liste pédagogique (10 % chacun). Jamais de catalogue.

Ce que ça veut dire pour Uromend

  • C'est le concurrent à copier intelligemment : même cible, même promesse, 10 × le volume. Leur meilleure accroche (« 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing… Number one: Stop taking Flomax ») peut devenir une vidéo Uromend avec un vrai visage.
  • Leur attaque contre les poudres (« heat-processed powder that wrecks the beta-sitosterol ») vise directement les formules en gélules comme Uromend : préparer la réponse (doses complètes, 6 actifs, bêta-sitostérol dosé séparément, pas « caché » dans une huile).
  • L'astuce une pub par médicament (Flomax, tamsulosin, finasteride, tadalafil, et aussi silodosin) est simple à reproduire : Uromend n'a qu'une pub « Say goodbye to weak and expensive Rx treatments ».
  • Le virage érection (page Christopher Jay) montre où va l'argent : prostate → performance sexuelle, même client. Uromend a l'ortie et le saw palmetto dans sa formule : un angle « libido/confiance » est possible sans changer de produit.
  • À surveiller au prochain comptage : si Veda Lockwood dépasse 1 500 pubs, ils scalent l'angle Flomax ; si ultimapeak grossit, ils pivotent.

Mise à jour du 19 octobre 2026 (relevé n°2)

  • Volume : 1 340 → 1 380 pubs actives (+3 %). Veda Lockwood stable à 1 000 ; Christopher Jay 340 → 380 (+12 %).
  • Christopher Jay bascule vers UltimaPeak : les 9 nouvelles pubs du 6 au 14 octobre vont toutes vers ultimapeak.com (gummies érection), plus aucune nouvelle pub prostate sur cette page. Récits à la première personne, homme de 47 à 53 ans, divorcé ou remarié, titres crus (« My Wife Stopped Trying To Have S*x With Me », « The Brutal Math Nobody Warned Me About »). Les pubs « Please don't buy Elivora Pumpkin Seed Oil » (20 sept) et « My father stopped buying pumpkin seed oil » ne sont plus visibles dans le haut de la page.
  • Veda Lockwood parle maintenant aux épouses : nouvelle pub du 12 octobre « Ladies, if your husband has to pull over at every gas station because he can't hold it, please read this » (signée Veda, infirmière, 56 ans). Même méthode qu'Everly : la femme lit, le mari prend le produit. Voir la pub.
  • Déclinaisons par médicament : « 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing » existe maintenant en version finastéride (8 oct) ; « My husband refused X for four years » tourne avec Flomax, tamsulosine et finastéride (7 oct). Les versions tamsulosine/finastéride du 7-8 oct ne sont plus en haut de page le 19 : elles tournent par rotation.
  • Classements : 4e sur « Flomax », 7e sur « enlarged prostate » (inchangé) ; monte d'une place sur « bladder support », « saw palmetto » et « pumpkin seed oil ».
  • Pour Uromend : l'angle « épouse » est désormais utilisé par les deux gros (Everly et Elivora). Une pub Uromend qui s'adresse à la femme (« Mesdames, si votre mari s'arrête à chaque station-service… ») est à tester, avec la vraie bouteille et la gélule en visuel.

Mise à jour du 25 octobre 2026 (relevé n°5) : deux pages cachées, la marque fait 2 740 pubs

  • Deux pages Facebook jamais suivies renvoient vers elivorahealth.com : « Dr. Greg Ellison » (1 200 pubs actives, pubs depuis le 3 septembre) et « Natural Health & Wellness Club » (250, depuis le 5 août). Total marque : 2 740 pubs (Veda Lockwood 1 100, Greg Ellison 1 200, NHWC 250, Christopher Jay 190), contre 1 190 comptées le 23 octobre. Elivora n'est plus « 10 fois Uromend », c'est 11 fois.
  • Le personnage Greg Ellison change selon la pub : « urologue depuis 17 ans », « depuis 21 ans », « 62 ans », « 66 ans, électricien retraité de Charlotte ». Même méthode que Veda Lockwood : un nom, des récits différents par douleur.
  • Angles de la page Greg Ellison (30 pubs lues, 0 vidéo, texte long + image) :

- La chirurgie refusée : « I've done TURP surgery on more than 400 men. When my colleague told me I was next, I took the referral slip home and put it in a drawer » ; « 65 to 75% of TURP patients lose normal ejaculation permanently » (voir). TURP = résection de la prostate par l'urètre ; HoLEP, Rezum, Aquablation = les autres opérations. La peur de l'opération remplace la peur de la sonde. - Les études retournées : « The NIH tested pumpkin seed oil twice. Both times it lost to sugar pills. The second time they tripled the dose. It still lost » (voir) et la version saw palmetto sur l'autre page. Ils attaquent leur propre ingrédient pour dire que seule leur forme (huile pressée à froid) marche. - L'argent gaspillé : « I spent about $700 on prostate supplements over 2 years and went from 1 trip a night to 3 » ; « 847 prostate supplements listed on Amazon… fewer than a handful are formulated in a way that allows the active compounds to reach the prostate ». - La transparence : « This month we received over 800 refund requests » et « products have appeared on Amazon, at Walmart… packaged closely enough that our customers could not tell the difference » (lutte contre les copies = preuve de succès). - Le médecin provocateur : « I'm about to piss off every urologist, GP, and pharmaceutical rep in this country » ; « If your PSA just came back high and you're up at midnight googling… ». - Prostate + érection : « Nearly 7 out of 10 men with an enlarged prostate also struggle with their erections ».

  • La page Natural Health & Wellness Club recycle les textes des autres pages, plus : « The last time I was inside my wife, I finished and nothing came out » (éjaculation rétrograde due au Flomax), le récit de l'épouse « He came home with a catheter bag strapped to his leg » (texte identique à Everly) et la liste de symptômes mot pour mot de Protaflo (« Waking up 2, 3… even 5 times a night »). Même agence ou copie directe.
  • Pages de vente : 18 pubs → elivorahealth.com/pages/prostate, 12 → /pages/7-reasons-pumpkin-prostate-health (NHWC : 22 vers « 7 reasons »).
  • Pour Uromend : l'ennemi n°1 d'Elivora passe du Flomax à la chirurgie et aux autres compléments (y compris la courge en poudre). Uromend est directement visé par « pumpkin seed oil lost to sugar pills ». Réponse : les doses (1 400 mg) et l'étiquette complète, et pourquoi pas une pub qui cite la même étude NIH pour expliquer la différence de dose.

Mise à jour du 27 octobre 2026 (relevé n°6) : -34 %, et deux nouveaux angles

  • 2 740 → 1 800 pubs actives (-34 %) : Veda Lockwood 1 100 → 640, Christopher Jay 190 → 0, Greg Ellison 1 200 → 1 000, Natural Health & Wellness Club 250 → 160. Sur « Flomax », Elivora passe de la 1re à la 16e place ; c'est Purely Nutrient qui prend la tête. Pendant ce temps Everly est revenu à 9 840 pubs. Trafic Similarweb : elivorahealth.com +1 576 % sur le mois (septembre), rang mondial 1 133 064, 89,6 % US.
  • Nouvel angle PSA (23 octobre, 3 variantes) : « If your PSA came back high, your biopsy came back clear, and your doctor said "we'll keep an eye on it," read this before your next recheck… PSA was 5.8… He came back. PSA was 7.2 » (voir). PSA = la prise de sang qui surveille la prostate ; la pub vise l'homme qui a eu peur du cancer, a été rassuré (BPH = prostate qui gonfle, pas cancer) et s'est fait dire « on surveille » sans solution.
  • Nouvel angle « copies » (16 octobre) : « Since we launched Elivora, products have appeared on Amazon, at Walmart… all of them packaged and labeled closely enough… People blamed Elivora. But the product they were holding was not Elivora… why does Elivora say two softgels per day when almost every other pumpkin seed product tells you to take one or three dry capsules? » (voir). La gélule sèche est présentée comme la version qui ne marche pas.
  • Veda Lockwood : le faux avertissement « There are some side effects of pumpkin seed oil that you should be warned about… Your nighttime bathroom trips may suddenly drop from four or five down to one » (voir) et « Here are the most dangerous foods for your prostate. I do not care if you do not believe me. I have spent 14 years on a urology floor » (5 variantes, 7-8 octobre, voir). Et la pub « 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing » existe maintenant en version « Stop taking tamsulosin » (voir).
  • Pour Uromend : l'ennemi d'Elivora est maintenant la gélule sèche (notre format) et l'homme « sous surveillance PSA » (un public que personne d'autre ne vise). Deux idées : la pub « Compare the label » (doses et 6 ingrédients) et une pub PSA honnête : « Your PSA is "being watched". Here's what you can do in the meantime » (sans promesse sur le PSA lui-même).

In one sentence

Elivora is Uromend's closest competitor: pumpkin seed + saw palmetto + beta-sitosterol, "Stop taking Flomax" angle, ≈ 1,340 active ads on 2 pages (Veda Lockwood 1,000, Christopher Jay 340), almost all launched in October 2026 (45 of the 60 collected): they are in full acceleration. And since early October the Christopher Jay page has been testing a second product, erection gummies (ultimapeak.com).

Marketing angles (60 ads read)

  • "Stop taking Flomax" leads: "I'm a urologist, and here are 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing immediately if they keep getting up to pee at night. Number one: Stop taking Flomax" (4th on the "Flomax" search, 18th on "bladder support"). Flomax "relaxes the muscle" but "doesn't shrink what is swollen"; only "the one cold-pressed oil" does.
  • The husband who refused the drug: "My husband refused Flomax / tamsulosin / finasteride / tadalafil for four years. Here's what happened to his prostate": the same ad exists with 4 different drug names (Oct 7, 2026) to capture each search. Narrator: a nurse of 28 years who "catheterized men who couldn't empty their own bladders".
  • The 3 a.m. fall: "Flomax had already put him face down on the hallway floor at 3 a.m., bleeding from a split eyebrow" (a real Flomax side effect: orthostatic hypotension, dizziness when standing up). Very concrete angle.
  • Dangerous foods for the prostate: "Here are the most dangerous foods for your prostate. I do not care if you do not believe me". List format.
  • The beer given up for nothing: "Too many men who poured their beer down the sink… and still got up three times a night". Speaks to the guy who already made efforts.
  • Founder story: "My father stopped buying pumpkin seed oil because none of it ever worked. So I flew to Styria to source the real thing" (Styria = a region of Austria, the "reference" pumpkin seed). And the fake confession "Please don't buy Elivora… We didn't warn you what it feels like to sleep through the night again".
  • "We lied": "We need to publicly apologize… because we lied. Yes, we're using cold-pressed virgin pumpkin seed oil, not the heat-processed powder that wrecks the fragile beta-sitosterol and delta-7 sterols": they attack powders (so pumpkin-powder capsules like… Uromend).
  • Kidneys / eGFR (July 2026, inherited from Everly): "I need to tell you something that might piss you off about your nephrologist".
  • New since October: erections (Christopher Jay page, 19 ads to ultimapeak.com): "What Happens When Your New Wife Wants Your D*ck More Than You Can Give?", "I've been lying to men about their ED for 15 years", "My Husband's Doctor Gave Him Viagra. Six Months Later He Was Dead", a "senior Pfizer researcher" who quits. Very crude language, stories of remarriage at 51 to a younger woman.

Target

  • Men aged 50 to 70 on Flomax/tamsulosin, 3 to 6 night trips, afraid of the catheter ("thread a tube into themselves three, four, five times a day") and of surgery.
  • Their wives/nurses as narrators ("My husband…", "I prepped men for prostate surgery for 31 years").
  • Since October: men aged 47 to 58, divorced or remarried with erection problems, and their wives ("For any woman whose husband is brushing off his erection problems").

Promises

  • Going from 3-6 night trips to 0-1 "in 12 weeks" / "four weeks after… he was getting up once a night".
  • "Shrinks what is swollen": a stronger promise than "supports".
  • Avoid the catheter, surgery, Flomax dizziness.
  • Gummies: erections "without the blue pill".

Offers and landing pages

  • elivorahealth.com/pages/prostate (26 ads): main advertorial.
  • elivorahealth.com/pages/7-reasons-pumpkin-prostate-health (13 ads): "7 reasons" page, list format.
  • ultimapeak.com/products/performance-gummies-remake (19 ads): the new erection product.
  • Product: Elivora Pumpkin Seed Oil softgels (Styrian oil, cold-pressed) + saw palmetto.

Formats

  • Almost 100% image + long copy (only 1 video out of 60): a "proof" photo (man in bed, bottle, prescription) + a 3,000-character story signed by a fake urologist ("Dr. Veda Lockwood", 23 years of practice) or a nurse.
  • The ads are copied word for word from Everly ("My husband refused Flomax for four years", "My Husband Was Getting Up 6 Times a Night", "We need to publicly apologize"): same team or same template.
  • Variants per keyword (Flomax / tamsulosin / finasteride / tadalafil): one ad per searched drug.

What the videos say

  • Only one video collected (Christopher Jay page, Oct 14, 2026, the "senior Pfizer researcher" story for the gummies): empty transcript, it's a video without voice (on-screen text + music). Elivora is a 100% long copy + image brand: the whole pitch is in the ads below, readable in full.

Hooks, awareness level, personas, enemies, narrative (indicative figures from 10/17; the charts below are recalculated at each snapshot)

  • #1 hook: the wife's story (40% of ads): "My husband refused Flomax / tamsulosin / finasteride / tadalafil for four years". Then authority ("I'm a urologist…", 10%), fake confession ("Please don't buy Elivora", "we lied", 10%), list ("3 things men over 50 need to stop doing", 8%).
  • Awareness level (Schwartz): they mostly speak to the problem aware (50%: the man knows he gets up at night, that he's on Flomax) and the unaware (35%: "here's what your urologist doesn't tell you"). Almost never the "most aware" (price/promo: 2%): Elivora runs no promos in its ads, it educates and scares.
  • Persona: the man aged 50-70 on medication (63%), then the erection man (15%, new in October), the wife (12%).
  • Enemy: the drug in 95% of ads (Flomax first, tamsulosin = its generic, finasteride, tadalafil, silodosin). Not DHT, not other brands: the enemy is the prescription.
  • Narrative: the first-person testimonial (40%), then the expert story and the educational list (10% each). Never a catalog.

What it means for Uromend

  • It's the competitor to copy smartly: same target, same promise, 10 × the volume. Their best hook ("3 things men over 50 need to stop doing… Number one: Stop taking Flomax") can become a Uromend video with a real face.
  • Their attack on powders ("heat-processed powder that wrecks the beta-sitosterol") directly targets capsule formulas like Uromend: prepare the answer (full doses, 6 actives, beta-sitosterol dosed separately, not "hidden" in an oil).
  • The one ad per drug trick (Flomax, tamsulosin, finasteride, tadalafil, and also silodosin) is easy to replicate: Uromend has only one ad "Say goodbye to weak and expensive Rx treatments".
  • The erection pivot (Christopher Jay page) shows where the money goes: prostate → sexual performance, same customer. Uromend has nettle and saw palmetto in its formula: a "libido/confidence" angle is possible without changing the product.
  • To watch at the next count: if Veda Lockwood goes above 1,500 ads, they are scaling the Flomax angle; if ultimapeak grows, they are pivoting.

Update of October 19, 2026 (snapshot #2)

  • Volume: 1,340 → 1,380 active ads (+3%). Veda Lockwood stable at 1,000; Christopher Jay 340 → 380 (+12%).
  • Christopher Jay is switching to UltimaPeak: the 9 new ads from Oct 6 to 14 all go to ultimapeak.com (erection gummies), no new prostate ads on that page. First-person stories, men aged 47 to 53, divorced or remarried, crude headlines ("My Wife Stopped Trying To Have S*x With Me", "The Brutal Math Nobody Warned Me About"). The ads "Please don't buy Elivora Pumpkin Seed Oil" (Sept 20) and "My father stopped buying pumpkin seed oil" are no longer visible at the top of the page.
  • Veda Lockwood now speaks to wives: new ad from Oct 12 "Ladies, if your husband has to pull over at every gas station because he can't hold it, please read this" (signed Veda, nurse, 56). Same method as Everly: the wife reads, the husband takes the product. See the ad.
  • Variants per drug: "3 things men over 50 need to stop doing" now exists in a finasteride version (Oct 8); "My husband refused X for four years" runs with Flomax, tamsulosin and finasteride (Oct 7). The tamsulosin/finasteride versions from Oct 7-8 are no longer at the top of the page on the 19th: they rotate.
  • Rankings: 4th on "Flomax", 7th on "enlarged prostate" (unchanged); up one place on "bladder support", "saw palmetto" and "pumpkin seed oil".
  • For Uromend: the "wife" angle is now used by both big players (Everly and Elivora). A Uromend ad addressed to the woman ("Ladies, if your husband pulls over at every gas station…") is worth testing, with the real bottle and capsule as visuals.

Update of October 25, 2026 (snapshot #5): two hidden pages, the brand runs 2,740 ads

  • Two Facebook pages never tracked before send to elivorahealth.com: "Dr. Greg Ellison" (1,200 active ads, running since September 3) and "Natural Health & Wellness Club" (250, since August 5). Brand total: 2,740 ads (Veda Lockwood 1,100, Greg Ellison 1,200, NHWC 250, Christopher Jay 190), versus 1,190 counted on October 23. Elivora is no longer "10 times Uromend", it is 11 times.
  • The Greg Ellison character changes from ad to ad: "urologist for 17 years", "for 21 years", "62 years old", "66, retired electrician from Charlotte". Same method as Veda Lockwood: one name, a different story per pain point.
  • Angles of the Greg Ellison page (30 ads read, 0 videos, long text + image):

- The refused surgery: "I've done TURP surgery on more than 400 men. When my colleague told me I was next, I took the referral slip home and put it in a drawer"; "65 to 75% of TURP patients lose normal ejaculation permanently" (see). TURP = prostate resection through the urethra; HoLEP, Rezum, Aquablation = the other procedures. Fear of surgery replaces fear of the catheter. - Studies turned around: "The NIH tested pumpkin seed oil twice. Both times it lost to sugar pills. The second time they tripled the dose. It still lost" (see) and the saw palmetto version on the other page. They attack their own ingredient to say only their form (cold-pressed oil) works. - Wasted money: "I spent about $700 on prostate supplements over 2 years and went from 1 trip a night to 3"; "847 prostate supplements listed on Amazon… fewer than a handful are formulated in a way that allows the active compounds to reach the prostate". - Transparency: "This month we received over 800 refund requests" and "products have appeared on Amazon, at Walmart… packaged closely enough that our customers could not tell the difference" (fighting copies = proof of success). - The provocative doctor: "I'm about to piss off every urologist, GP, and pharmaceutical rep in this country"; "If your PSA just came back high and you're up at midnight googling…". - Prostate + erection: "Nearly 7 out of 10 men with an enlarged prostate also struggle with their erections".

  • The Natural Health & Wellness Club page recycles the other pages' copy, plus: "The last time I was inside my wife, I finished and nothing came out" (retrograde ejaculation caused by Flomax), the wife's story "He came home with a catheter bag strapped to his leg" (identical to Everly's text) and Protaflo's symptom list word for word ("Waking up 2, 3… even 5 times a night"). Same agency or direct copy.
  • Sales pages: 18 ads → elivorahealth.com/pages/prostate, 12 → /pages/7-reasons-pumpkin-prostate-health (NHWC: 22 to "7 reasons").
  • For Uromend: Elivora's enemy #1 shifts from Flomax to surgery and to other supplements (including powdered pumpkin seed). Uromend is directly targeted by "pumpkin seed oil lost to sugar pills". Answer: the doses (1,400 mg) and the full label, and why not an ad quoting the same NIH study to explain the dose difference.

Update of October 27, 2026 (snapshot #6): -34%, and two new angles

  • 2,740 → 1,800 active ads (-34%): Veda Lockwood 1,100 → 640, Christopher Jay 190 → 0, Greg Ellison 1,200 → 1,000, Natural Health & Wellness Club 250 → 160. On "Flomax", Elivora goes from 1st to 16th; Purely Nutrient takes the lead. Meanwhile Everly is back to 9,840 ads. Similarweb traffic: elivorahealth.com +1,576% month over month (September), global rank 1,133,064, 89.6% US.
  • New PSA angle (October 23, 3 variants): "If your PSA came back high, your biopsy came back clear, and your doctor said 'we'll keep an eye on it,' read this before your next recheck… PSA was 5.8… He came back. PSA was 7.2" (see). PSA = the blood test that monitors the prostate; the ad targets the man who feared cancer, was reassured (BPH = enlarged prostate, not cancer) and was told "we'll monitor it" with no solution.
  • New "counterfeits" angle (October 16): "Since we launched Elivora, products have appeared on Amazon, at Walmart… all of them packaged and labeled closely enough… People blamed Elivora. But the product they were holding was not Elivora… why does Elivora say two softgels per day when almost every other pumpkin seed product tells you to take one or three dry capsules?" (see). The dry capsule is presented as the version that does not work.
  • Veda Lockwood: the fake warning "There are some side effects of pumpkin seed oil that you should be warned about… Your nighttime bathroom trips may suddenly drop from four or five down to one" (see) and "Here are the most dangerous foods for your prostate. I do not care if you do not believe me. I have spent 14 years on a urology floor" (5 variants, October 7-8, see). And the "3 things men over 50 need to stop doing" ad now exists as "Stop taking tamsulosin" (see).
  • For Uromend: Elivora's enemy is now the dry capsule (our format) and the man "under PSA watch" (an audience nobody else targets). Two ideas: the "Compare the label" ad (doses and 6 ingredients) and an honest PSA ad: "Your PSA is 'being watched'. Here's what you can do in the meantime" (no promise on PSA itself).

Christopher Jay · 🎬 vidéovideo · débutstarted 14 oct 2026 · depuis 13 jrunning 13 days · rang 6 dans la pagerank 6 on the page (étaitwas 16)
In 2003, a senior Pfizer researcher named Dr. Donald Mitchell walked away from a $340,000 salary and 19 years with the company. In his exit interview, he said something that should terrify every man taking ED pills: "We know what actually restores erectile function. We're just not allowed to sell it because you can't patent a natural solution.” Today, Donald is 71 years old. He hasn't taken Viagra…
In 2003, a senior Pfizer researcher named Dr. Donald Mitchell walked away from a $340,000 salary and 19 years with the company.
In his exit interview, he said something that should terrify every man taking ED pills: "We know what actually restores erectile function. We're just not allowed to sell it because you can't patent a natural solution.”
Today, Donald is 71 years old.
He hasn't taken Viagra in 18 years.
And according to his wife Catherine (who agreed to be interviewed), they're still having sex 2-3 times per day. Yes… per day.
"Most of our friends stopped having sex in their 60s," Catherine said. "Their husbands are on pills that barely work. Donald does something different."
What Donald discovered during his time at Pfizer changed everything he thought he knew about erectile dysfunction. And it has nothing to do with the blue pill.
The Pfizer Files: What They Found (And Buried)
During Donald's tenure in Pfizer's vascular research division, the team was tasked with understanding why Viagra worked for some men but failed for others.
The drug worked by forcing blood vessels to dilate temporarily. But in clinical trials, 30-40% of men saw zero improvement.
The drug forced their vessels open, but blood still wouldn't flow properly.
Donald's team discovered why: These men had depleted levels of a molecule called nitric oxide.
Nitric oxide is the molecule that signals your blood vessels to relax, open up, and fill with blood naturally. After age 40, your body produces roughly 10% less every year. By 55, you've lost nearly half of what you had at 30. By 65, you're running on fumes.
Without nitric oxide, your blood vessels stay constricted. Blood can't flow freely. Not to your penis. Not to your brain. Not anywhere.
Viagra tries to force the plumbing open. But if you are experiencing Nitric Oxide Collapse, the signal never gets sent. It's like trying to open a door by pushing harder when the lock is broken. That’s why it stops working over time and why men still go soft halfway through, even on the pill.
The Discovery Pfizer Refused to Pursue
Donald's team found that men with restored nitric oxide levels responded dramatically better to ED treatment. Most didn't need the pill at all once their nitric oxide production was reactivated.
So Donald proposed a study: What if we help men restore nitric oxide naturally before prescribing Viagra?
The proposal was rejected within 48 hours.
"They told me there's no business model in solving the problem," Donald said in a 2019 interview. "Viagra generates $2 billion annually. If men restore nitric oxide naturally and don't need the drug anymore, that revenue disappears."
Donald kept digging on his own time. He found research from natural endocrinologists, clinical trials, and vascular studies.
All pointing to the same breakthrough: Your body can stop Nitric Oxide Collapse and reactive blood flow by using specific, naturally occurring compounds.
Here's the mechanism: Instead of forcing blood through a broken system, you can supply your body with exact ingredients specially made for a man to restore his nitric oxide production directly at the source.
The research proved it works: Specific adaptogens drop the hormones that cut off nitric oxide, while others rebuild cellular energy and sustain blood flow.
And when your body has the nitric oxide it needs… Blood vessels relax naturally. Blood flow returns to normal. Erections happen the way they're supposed to.
No forced dilation. No pill windows. No side effects. Just your body working the way it did when you were 30.
Why Your Doctor Never Told You This
Donald left Pfizer in 2003 because he couldn't ethically keep prescribing a drug when he knew there was a better solution. But when he started telling patients about natural nitric oxide restoration, he faced immediate pushback from the medical establishment.
"I had colleagues tell me I was being irresponsible," he said. "Because I was suggesting natural elements over pharmaceuticals."

Veda Lockwood · 🖼 image · débutstarted 29 juil 2026 · depuis 90 jrunning 90 days · rang 6 dans la pagerank 6 on the page (étaitwas 9)
If you're currently taking Lasix, lisinopril, drinking dandelion tea, or watching your protein intake just to keep your kidney numbers from sliding every quarter… I'm asking you, please read this before your next blood draw. Because what I'm about to share with you could be the difference between getting your filtration stabilizing naturally again within 8 weeks… and ending up in my office in 5 years s…
If you're currently taking Lasix, lisinopril, drinking dandelion tea, or watching your protein intake just to keep your kidney numbers from sliding every quarter… I'm asking you, please read this before your next blood draw.
Because what I'm about to share with you could be the difference between getting your filtration stabilizing naturally again within 8 weeks… and ending up in my office in 5 years sitting across from me as I explain what a dialysis fistula is and where they'll place it in your arm.
My name is Dr. Sandra Holloway, and I've spent 21 years as a board-certified nephrologist specializing in chronic kidney decline in adults over 50.
And in those 21 years, I have watched the same heartbreaking pattern play out in my clinic over and over again.
A patient walks in at 55. Their primary care doctor flagged a slightly low eGFR. A little protein in the urine. Nothing serious.
They get told to "watch their blood pressure" and come back in a year. It's been fine.
Five years later, they're back. Now their eGFR has dropped from 75 to 50.
Their creatinine is climbing. They tell me their urine "just foams now no matter what I do."
Five years after that, they're in stage 4. Sitting across from me as I draw a diagram of a fistula.
Swelling in both ankles they can press a thumbprint into. Up four times a night.
They're frightened and ashamed.
Five years after THAT… I'm coordinating their dialysis schedule.
Because by that point, their nephrons have stopped responding to anything at all. They're tied to a machine three days a week. They can't travel, can't eat what they want, can't go a single day without planning around it.
And every one of these patients tells me the same thing:
"Doctor, I wish someone had told me what was actually happening to me 15 years ago."
Well that's what I'm doing right now. I'm telling you.
Because if you're currently relying on water pills, blood pressure meds, or diet alone to keep your numbers from sliding, you are walking that exact same path right now whether you realize it or not.
And it's not because your kidneys are "just old." It's not because you "didn't catch it early enough."
It's something far more dangerous than that.
Let me explain.
You see, most doctors will tell you that declining kidney function is simply irreversible. They'll tell you the goal is just to "slow it down."
They'll tell you to manage your blood pressure and wait.
And on paper, that's the standard of care.
But what they don't tell you… because most of them never look past the blood pressure number… is that while you're managing your blood pressure, the actual driver of your filtration loss is silently accelerating inside your kidneys every single day.
And that driver has nothing to do with how much water you're drinking. Nothing to do with how much salt you cut.
Nothing to do with how carefully you're eating.
It has to do with a hidden cycle of chronic inflammation scarring your filters — one that almost every adult over 55 with declining numbers has, but almost no patient has ever been told about.
Let me walk you through what's actually happening inside you right now.
👉 The first thing that's going on is that you don't have a few filters in each kidney. You have about a million.
These are your nephrons. Each one is a microscopic filter, and together they clean your entire blood supply around 30 times a day.
But here's what nobody explains. Nephrons don't regenerate. When one scars over, it's gone for good.
For most adults with declining numbers, these filters have been quietly scarring for years. Sometimes it accelerated after years of high blood pressure. Sometimes it's decades of low-grade inflammation, blood sugar, medications, or all of it together.
Whatever the trigger was, the damage works the same way. The delicate filtering membranes get inflamed. Inflamed tissue scars. Scarred nephrons shut down.

I'm a nurse with Stage 3 kidney disease. I tried everything. Here's what actually worked.
I've been a nurse for 38 years, and kidney disease is one of the hardest things I see my people struggle with.
And I do mean MY people. Black Americans are three times more likely to develop kidney failure than white Americans. Three times. I've watched it take aunties, uncles, fathers, church mothers, neighbors. The foamy urine. The burning when you pee. The swollen ankles that never go down. I've had patients tell me they're up 3 or 4 times every night running to the bathroom. Day and night, it never stops.
About three years ago, it happened to me. My own kidneys started declining out of control. I have stage 3 kidney disease. All those years of long shifts, putting everybody else first, not taking care of myself the way I should have finally caught up with me.
Because that's what we do, isn't it? We the ones holding everybody else together. The strong ones. The ones who don't complain. I spent 38 years taking care of other people's health and completely neglected my own.
At first it was just feeling tired after lunch. Then came the swelling. Those sudden waves of exhaustion and brain fog that hit you out of nowhere. By the end of my shift, I could barely think straight. But the worst part? Nighttime. The second I'd finally fall asleep, I'd wake up needing to pee. Again. And again. I'd be dragging myself to the bathroom three, sometimes four times a night, feeling exhausted no matter how much water I drank.
Now I was the one living with it. And let me tell you, every patient was right. It's a living hell.
My doctor looked at my numbers and said, "Well, you're stage 3, but it's not critical yet. Let's just keep monitoring. Drink more water, cut your sodium." That was it. I sat in that parking lot for ten minutes just staring at my steering wheel.
Now let me tell you something us Black folks already know in our bones. The medical system was not built for us. From Tuskegee to today, we have been undertreated, dismissed, and told our pain isn't real. I've watched it happen to my patients for decades. And here I was — a registered nurse with 38 years of experience — being told to "just monitor" while my kidneys declined.
Monitor. Like I hadn't already watched this same script play out in my community a hundred times.
I asked about other options. He mentioned dialysis as something we might consider "down the road." Like it was no big deal. Chile, I have watched too many of my people struggle through that process. The fistula surgery that takes months to heal. The three-times-a-week schedule that takes over your entire life. I wasn't about to sit there and let that become my story without fighting first.
So I started looking into natural options. Cranberry supplements. Kidney support blends. Cutting protein completely. Nothing really worked. My sister found out I was spending money on supplements and said "girl, you turning into one of those people." She told me to just listen to my doctor. Easy for her to say — she's not the one waking up four times a night wondering if her kidneys are going to give out.
Sis, I hear you. But I've watched too many Black women accept "just monitor" as a final answer and end up on dialysis five years later. That wasn't going to be me.
Then about two months ago, one of my regular patients came in for her appointment. Sweet Black woman, mid-60s, stage 3 kidney disease and declining function for years. She'd been struggling with it for over a decade. I always felt terrible for her because she'd tried everything and nothing gave her more than a few points of stable eGFR.
But this time she seemed different. Lighter somehow. Glowing, actually. Like the weight she'd been carrying had shifted.
I asked her how she was managing, and she said something I didn't expect. She told me her kidney function actually wasn't declining anymore. She'd found cold-pressed pumpkin seed oil paired with saw palmetto and started taking it

I almost had a heart attack on a Caribbean cruise. The ship's doctor was from Switzerland. She asked me ONE question that made my American doctor look 15 years behind.
I'm 64 years old.
My husband and I had been saving for this trip for almost three years. Caribbean cruise. Ten days. Puerto Rico, St. Thomas, St. Lucia.
We'd talked about this trip since the kids were in college. The kind of trip you plan in your forties for your sixties. The reason you keep showing up to a job you stopped loving years ago.
Day four, we're walking up the cobblestone streets of Charlotte Amalie toward Blackbeard's Castle for sunset.
You've seen the pictures. White and coral colonial buildings climbing the hillside. Red tin rooftops in the sun. The turquoise Caribbean spread out below. Cobblestones underfoot worn smooth by two hundred years of Danish, then American, then tourist feet.
The kind of place you put on a wall when you finally get home.
My husband was walking ahead of me. He had his phone out taking pictures. Laughing at something a vendor had said in broken English.
I was about thirty feet behind him.
I'd noticed the climbing starting to get to me about halfway up. My heart was pounding hard. Not just the regular hard you'd expect on a hill. The deep pounding that I'd been feeling at home for the last six months walking up the stairs to our bedroom.
I'd been telling myself it was just getting older. Just being out of shape from a winter of sitting at a desk.
I'd been telling my husband the same thing.
I stopped on the cobblestones to catch my breath. Put my hand on a stone wall. Tried to slow my breathing.
It didn't slow.
The pounding got worse. My chest started to feel tight, not painful exactly, but like someone was pressing a hand against my sternum. I started to sweat through my blouse. I felt the blood drain out of my face.
I sat down on the wall.
I tried to call out to my husband but my voice wouldn't come.
Tourists kept walking around me. Stepping over my feet. Taking pictures of the view behind me.
I sat there with my hand on my chest thinking about my father.
My father died of a heart attack at 72. He was sitting at his kitchen table on a Sunday morning reading the sports section. He just slumped forward.
I was 39 when that happened.
And here I was, 64 years old, on a Caribbean cobblestone wall two thousand miles from home, doing the same thing.
My husband turned around.
I will never forget his face. He'd been laughing one second. The next second he dropped his phone on the cobblestones and ran back to me.
'Caroline. Caroline what's happening.'
I couldn't speak.
He grabbed my arm. Yelled for someone to help. A local man in his sixties from a nearby cafe came running with a chair. They got me into the chair. Somebody brought water.
The cafe owner called for a vehicle. We're in the old town of Charlotte Amalie. The streets are too narrow for ambulances to move fast. They had a small cart that could navigate the cobblestones. They got me onto it and drove us down to the port.
My husband held my hand the whole way down. He didn't say anything. Just held my hand and looked at me with the face I had been seeing in nightmares for twenty-five years since my father died.
We got onto the tender boat back to the ship. He kept his hand on my chest the whole way across the water.
I told him I was okay.
I wasn't.
The ship's doctor was waiting for us when we got on board. Someone had radioed ahead.
Her name was Dr. Steiner. Swiss accent. White hair cut short. Maybe early sixties.
She had me lie down on the exam table in the medical center on Deck 3. Hooked me up to a monitor. Took my blood pressure twice. Frowned at the reading. Drew blood. Ran a finger-prick glucose. Looked at the numbers.
'You did not have a heart attack today,' she said. 'But you came close.'
I was lying there with my husband sitting next to me holding my hand. I just looked at the ceiling.
'Your blood pressure is 178 over 104. Your pulse is irregular. Your blood sugar is

Veda Lockwood · 🖼 image · débutstarted 20 août 2026 · depuis 68 jrunning 68 days · rang 2 dans la pagerank 2 on the page (étaitwas 2)
My husband refused Flomax for four years. Here's what happened to his prostate. I've been a nurse for 28 years. I've catheterized men who couldn't empty their own bladders. I've charted post-op notes on men whose prostates had swollen so large they needed surgery just to urinate. My husband refused Flomax for four years. Here's what happened to his prostate.
I've been a nurse for 28 years. I've catheterized men who couldn't empty their own bladders. I've charted post-op notes on men whose prostates had swollen so large they needed surgery just to urinate. I've watched patient after patient come back every three months with the same complaints—the urgency, the weak stream, the getting up four, five, six times a night—while their doctors adjusted the dose and said, "Give it time."
Then my husband started getting up every ninety minutes. And I realized "give it time" was the most dangerous phrase in urology.
I need to tell you about my father first. Because until you understand what I watched happen to him, nothing else I'm about to say will make sense.
My father was diagnosed with an enlarged prostate at 58. His doctor started him on tamsulosin immediately. 0.4 milligrams. Once daily. He was a good patient. The kind who never missed a dose. He cut back on coffee. Stopped drinking anything after 7 PM. Did everything they told him.
His symptoms improved slightly. His urologist smiled at the flow test. "Looking better, Frank. Stay the course."
Stay the course.
For eleven years, he stayed the course. Eleven years of tamsulosin. Eleven years of "manageable symptoms." Eleven years of his urologist looking at a chart and calling it a success.
But the side effects were never manageable. The dizziness started in year two—he couldn't stand up without grabbing the wall. The fatigue was constant. He'd fall asleep in his recliner at 6 PM like someone had unplugged him. And the one thing he never talked about—the thing my mother only told me about after he was gone—was that the medication destroyed their intimacy. Completely. For the last eight years of his life, they slept in the same bed like roommates.
And his prostate kept growing. While the medication relaxed the muscles around his bladder, the gland itself never stopped swelling. Because tamsulosin doesn't shrink the prostate. It doesn't stop whatever is causing the growth. It just forces the muscles to loosen so urine can squeeze past the obstruction.
Like putting a bigger doorframe around a door that's still being blocked. The obstruction never moves. It just gets bigger. And eventually, the bigger doorframe isn't enough.
At 67, my father couldn't urinate at all. Acute urinary retention. They rushed him to the ER and inserted a catheter. He came home with a bag strapped to his leg. For five weeks, my father—a man who built his own deck, who coached Little League, who never let anyone see him struggle—walked around with a catheter bag under his pants. He stopped going to church. He stopped going to the hardware store. He sat in his recliner and watched the bag fill.
The TURP surgery was supposed to fix everything. They scraped out the prostate tissue that was blocking his urethra. The surgeon called it "routine." My father came home wearing adult diapers. The incontinence was supposed to be temporary. It wasn't.
He leaked for the rest of his life. Every cough. Every laugh. Every time he stood up from a chair. The man who built things with his hands was now carrying spare underwear in a plastic bag whenever he left the house.
He stopped leaving the house.
He died at 71. Not from the prostate. From pneumonia. But I'll tell you what killed him—it was the giving up. A man who quits going outside, quits moving, quits seeing friends because he's terrified of leaking through his pants in public—that man's immune system collapses. My father didn't die of pneumonia. He died of eleven years on a medication that masked the symptom while the actual problem destroyed his body from the inside.
Three years ago, my husband Robert started getting up at night. Once at first. Then twice. Then four times. Then every ninety minutes like clockwork.
I'd lie in bed listening to him get up. The mattress shifting. His feet on the floor. The bathroom light clicking on. The stream that so

I Took Pumpkin Seed Oil Every Single Morning For 11 Weeks. My Flow Never Improved. The Nighttime Trips Never Stopped. Then A Lab Technician Showed Me Why. My name is Greg Ellison. I'm 62 years old. I Took Pumpkin Seed Oil Every Single Morning For 11 Weeks. My Flow Never Improved. The Nighttime Trips Never Stopped. Then A Lab Technician Showed Me Why.
My name is Greg Ellison. I'm 62 years old.
I believed in pumpkin seed oil the way some people believe in prayer.
Not casually. Not as a "maybe this will help" afterthought.
I believed because I'd read the studies. Real ones. Researchers looking at the sterols in pumpkin seed oil — beta-sitosterol, the delta-7 sterols — and how they interfere with the DHT that drives prostate tissue to swell over time.
I'd read the research on how the prostate enlarges. I understood the pathway. It's not the testosterone you'd blame. It's DHT — a more aggressive hormone your body converts testosterone into using an enzyme called 5-alpha-reductase. DHT builds up in the prostate. The gland swells. It squeezes the urethra running through it like a hand tightening around a garden hose. And the flow slows to a trickle. The sterols in pumpkin seed oil interrupt that conversion and block DHT from binding. Take the pressure off the hose.
My PSA at my last panel: 4.2.
My flow had gone from a stream to a dribble over two years. I'd start, stop, start again. Never felt empty. My urgency was constant — I'd map every bathroom in every building I walked into. And one night I got up to go, stood there for a full minute waiting for it to start, and got back in bed. Then got up again twenty minutes later.
My father spent the last decade of his life planning his whole world around the nearest men's room. Then the catheter. Then the surgery that didn't fully fix it.
The science was clear. Pumpkin seed oil was my answer. I was sure of it.
My wife Linda found a health blog with dosing recommendations. Two tablespoons of cold-pressed pumpkin seed oil, mixed into a morning smoothie. We ordered a bottle from Amazon. Thirty-eight dollars.
Every single morning. Seven days a week. I measured out two tablespoons, poured them into my blender with a banana and almond milk, and hit the button.
The oil never really blended in. No matter how long I ran it. There was always a slick film that separated and floated to the top. A greasy, grassy layer that coated the inside of the glass and clung to the back of my throat.
The taste was nutty at first. Then bitter. Then just overwhelming. By week three, the smell of it in the morning made my stomach turn before I even poured the glass.
Every. Single. Morning.
Linda started making the smoothie some mornings when I left early for work. She'd text me a photo of the glass waiting in the fridge. A little heart emoji.
We were in this together.
I started keeping a log. Nighttime trips. Flow, strong or weak. How long I stood there waiting.
Week 1: Still up three times a night. No change.
"Give it time," Linda said. "The studies said it takes a few weeks."
Week 2: Flow unchanged. Still dribbling.
Week 3: Still standing at the toilet waiting for it to start. Still up three times a night.
Week 4: If anything the urgency was worse.
I stood at the toilet at 3 AM. Waited. Waited. Gave up. Got back in bed.
I sat at the kitchen table. The empty smoothie glass still had an oily ring at the bottom. I pushed it away.
"It takes longer," Linda said from the doorway. She didn't sound convinced.
Week 6: No change.
Week 8: Flow still weak.
Week 11: Still up three times a night.
Eleven weeks. Seventy-seven consecutive mornings. Over thirty dollars a week between the oil, the bananas, and the almond milk. A blender with a greasy film no amount of scrubbing could fully cut.
And I was still getting up three times a night. Every single night.
My next appointment was in ten weeks. My doctor had been clear at the last visit: "If your symptoms don't improve, we're going to talk about adding a medication. And Andre, depending on how the prostate looks, we may need to have a conversation about a procedure."
I knew what conversation he meant.
That night I didn't sleep. I lay in the dark and thought about my father.

I've treated men with severe benign prostatic hyperplasia. I've watched an enlarged prostate rob men of their sleep, their confidence, whatever dignity they had left standing at a urinal for t…
I put off the TURP surgery for 14 months. Then my flow test came back at 7 mL/sec and my colleague said, "We need to talk about resection."
I'm Dr. Greg Ellison. I've been a urologist for 21 years. I've treated men with severe benign prostatic hyperplasia. I've watched an enlarged prostate rob men of their sleep, their confidence, whatever dignity they had left standing at a urinal for two minutes and passing nothing. I've seen the trajectory from both sides of the chart.
So when my own PSA hit 4.2 and my flow rate dropped to 9, I knew exactly what my colleague was going to say before he said it.
"We should start talking about a TURP."
I smiled. I nodded. I took the referral slip.
And I threw it in the trash three days later.
Not because I'm reckless. Not because I'm in denial. But because I've scheduled that surgery for hundreds of men, and I've seen what comes after. The retrograde ejaculation nobody warns them about until it's done. The incontinence that doesn't always come back. The ones who need it done again in five years anyway.
Both roads lead somewhere I didn't want to go. I was trying to find a third option.
For fourteen months, I tried everything else. I cut caffeine completely. I stopped drinking anything after 7 p.m. I did the pelvic floor exercises. I took stinging nettle. I took pygeum. I took zinc. I took a saw palmetto capsule off the pharmacy shelf. I timed my fluids like a science experiment.
My flow came up from 9 to 11. My PSA settled to 3.8.
Better. But not good enough. Not even close.
And the cost? I was exhausted. I was still up three times a night. Still standing there at 3 a.m. waiting for a stream that trickled and stopped and started again. Still feeling like I never fully emptied, that heavy pressure that sat there an hour after I left the bathroom. I'd get four hours of broken sleep and drag myself through a full day of clinic seeing patients with the exact problem I couldn't fix in my own body.
I was doing everything right and my prostate was still squeezing that pipe shut.
My wife found me sitting on the edge of the tub one night at midnight, head in my hands.
"Just get the surgery," she said.
"I can't," I whispered. "I've done that surgery on hundreds of men. I know what they don't get told."
But I was running out of options.
Then something happened that changed everything.
I was reviewing a chart before a follow-up. Severe BPH, 66 years old, prostate volume 82 grams, IPSS symptom score of 27 at his last visit, already scheduled for surgery. A textbook resection candidate.
But this time, something was different.
He walked in lighter. Not just physically. He had that look men get back when they've finally slept. He was cracking jokes with my nurse in the hallway before I even opened the door.
I pulled up his flow test from that morning: 18 mL/sec. IPSS down to 11.
Three months ago: 8 mL/sec. IPSS of 27.
I looked up from the screen. "Frank. Your flow. Your symptom score. What changed?"
He grinned. "You're not gonna believe me."
"Try me."
"My daughter got me these softgels. Elivora. Cold-pressed pumpkin seed oil with saw palmetto. Took about ten days and I stopped getting up at night. By two months I was emptying all the way for the first time in years. Cancelled the surgery. My other doc ran the flow test twice because he didn't trust it."
I sat there staring at his chart.
"Are you telling me pumpkin seed oil took your flow from eight to eighteen?"
"I'm telling you I sleep through the night now. I don't dribble. I don't stand there waiting. And your office took the surgery off the table."
I went home that night and couldn't sleep. Not because I was up peeing. Because of what he'd said.
I've been a doctor for 21 years. I don't believe in magic supplements. I don't trust testimonials. I trust mechanisms and evidence.
So at 1:30 a.m., still awake, I started digging.
And what I found rearranged everything I thought I knew about prostate enlargement, even after practicing urology for two decades.
Most

This is what would happen to a man who was up four times a night if he took Elivora for 8 weeks.
After his first dose, his system absorbs cold-pressed pumpkin seed oil, rich in beta-sitosterol and delta-7 sterols, paired with saw palmetto. Together they go after the real problem behind the swelling. The DHT that's been squeezing his prostate tighter every year. The pressure no bathroom trip has ever relieved.
After 24 hours, nothing visible yet. Still getting up, still that full feeling. But inside, something's shifting. Saw palmetto is slowing the testosterone that converts into DHT. The signal that's been feeding the swelling for years is starting to quiet.
After one week, he feels it before he sees it. That constant urge that pulls him out of bed. Easing. The beta-sitosterol is blocking DHT at the receptor sites where it does the damage.
After four weeks, now he notices it. He sleeps through the night for the first time in years. The stream is stronger, the pressure is lifting. The delta-7 sterols are working downstream where the swelling presses on the pipe.
After eight weeks, he empties all the way and stays down till morning. Saw palmetto has kept the urinary pressure calmed so it doesn't creep back. The nights he used to dread are quiet again.
This is the power of cold-pressed pumpkin seed oil and saw palmetto working together on both fronts. Pure, potent, and delivered in oil-form softgels the way nature made them, not heat-stripped like the cheap versions.

I'm a urologist, and here are 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing immediately if they keep getting up to pee at night. Number one: Stop taking Flomax. My name is Dr. Veda Lockwood. In my 23 years as a board-certified urologist, I have watched thousands of men destroy their sleep, their energy, and their confidence using the exact same three methods to stop nighttime urination. I'm a urologist, and here are 3 things men over 50 need to stop doing immediately if they keep getting up to pee at night. Number one: Stop taking Flomax.
My name is Dr. Veda Lockwood. In my 23 years as a board-certified urologist, I have watched thousands of men destroy their sleep, their energy, and their confidence using the exact same three methods to stop nighttime urination. And every single one of those methods is actually making the swelling worse, weakening their bladder, and driving them straight toward a catheter.
If you are currently getting up two, three, four times a night to stand over the toilet with a weak, stop-and-start stream, here is a rapid-fire breakdown of exactly what you are doing wrong, and the one cold-pressed oil that actually shrinks what is swollen.
1. Stop Taking Flomax
Every guy thinks Flomax is a prostate fix because his doctor handed him the prescription. But Flomax is a relaxation drug, not a shrinking drug. All it does is force the smooth muscle around your urethra to relax so urine can squeeze past the swelling. It is a chemical bypass. It does absolutely nothing about the swelling itself.
Your prostate stays the same size. It keeps growing. And every year, you need a higher dose just to get the same trickle.
Meanwhile the side effects stack up silently. Dizziness when you stand. Retrograde ejaculation. Brain fog so thick you cannot remember where you put your keys.
And the moment you stop taking it, your stream slams shut worse than before because the underlying swelling has been growing unchecked the entire time you were on the drug.
2. Stop Drinking Cranberry Juice
This is the worst advice wives give their husbands, and I hear it every single week. Cranberry juice is an acid tool. It acidifies your urine to make it hostile to bacteria. That is helpful for a urinary tract infection.
But your problem is not an infection. Your problem is a prostate gland that has physically swollen to twice its normal size and is now squeezing your urethra shut like a fist around a garden hose.
Dumping acidic juice on a mechanical blockage does nothing except irritate your already-inflamed bladder wall, which makes your urgency WORSE. That is exactly why you feel like you have to go even more desperately after drinking it, even though almost nothing comes out.
3. Stop Ignoring WHY Your Prostate Is Swelling
Everyone treats nighttime urination like it is a bladder issue. It is not. Your bladder is just the messenger. The actual engine of the problem is sitting right underneath it.
Starting around age 40, your body begins converting more and more of your testosterone into a hormone called DHT through an enzyme called 5-alpha reductase. DHT is the single most powerful growth signal your prostate gland receives. Every year, more DHT means more growth. More growth means more compression on your urethra.
Here is the chain reaction that is wrecking your sleep:
DHT floods your prostate → the gland swells → your urethra gets squeezed → your bladder cannot fully empty → your kidneys back up fluid → your face gets puffy → your hair follicles starve → you wake up every 90 minutes to push out a weak, incomplete stream.
This is why Flomax fails. This is why cranberry juice fails. This is why "just stop drinking water after 7 PM" fails. None of them touch DHT. None of them touch 5-alpha reductase. None of them shrink a single millimeter of prostate tissue.
You are treating a swelling problem with a relaxation drug and a bacteria killer.
How to Actually Shrink What Is Swollen
You cannot fix a growing prostate with a muscle relaxer or cranberry acid. To actually reverse the swelling and get your nights back, you have to do two things simultaneously:
✅ Block the DHT: You need a natural 5-alpha reductase inhibitor to cut off the growth signal at the source, so your prostate stops getting bigger.
✅ Deliver Beta-Sitosterol: You need the specific plant sterol clinically shown to reduce prostate volume and improve urinary flow —

🎃 Still getting up 2-3 times a night to pee?
If you're a man over 50 dealing with:
🚽 Constant trips to the bathroom (especially at night)
💧 A weak stream that starts, stops, and won't finish
⏳ That "gotta go" urgency that hits out of nowhere
...it's not just "getting older." It's your prostate.
Here's what most men don't know: as DHT builds up, it drives the prostate to crowd the urethra — and that's what turns every bathroom trip into a battle.
✅ That's why thousands of men are reaching for cold-pressed Pumpkin Seed Oil — the beta-sitosterol and plant sterols inside target the real mechanism, not just the symptoms.

🎃 Still getting up 2-3 times a night to pee?
If you're a man over 50 dealing with:
🚽 Constant trips to the bathroom (especially at night)
💧 A weak stream that starts, stops, and won't finish
⏳ That "gotta go" urgency that hits out of nowhere
...it's not just "getting older." It's your prostate.
Here's what most men don't know: as DHT builds up, it drives the prostate to crowd the urethra — and that's what turns every bathroom trip into a battle.
✅ That's why thousands of men are reaching for cold-pressed Pumpkin Seed Oil — the beta-sitosterol and plant sterols inside target the real mechanism, not just the symptoms.

I Took Pumpkin Seed Oil Every Single Morning For 11 Weeks. My Flow Never Improved. The Nighttime Trips Never Stopped. Then A Lab Technician Showed Me Why.
My name is Greg Ellison. I'm 62 years old.
I believed in pumpkin seed oil the way some people believe in prayer.
Not casually. Not as a "maybe this will help" afterthought.
I believed because I'd read the studies. Real ones. Researchers looking at the sterols in pumpkin seed oil — beta-sitosterol, the delta-7 sterols — and how they interfere with the DHT that drives prostate tissue to swell over time.
I'd read the research on how the prostate enlarges. I understood the pathway. It's not the testosterone you'd blame. It's DHT — a more aggressive hormone your body converts testosterone into using an enzyme called 5-alpha-reductase. DHT builds up in the prostate. The gland swells. It squeezes the urethra running through it like a hand tightening around a garden hose. And the flow slows to a trickle. The sterols in pumpkin seed oil interrupt that conversion and block DHT from binding. Take the pressure off the hose.
My PSA at my last panel: 4.2.
My flow had gone from a stream to a dribble over two years. I'd start, stop, start again. Never felt empty. My urgency was constant — I'd map every bathroom in every building I walked into. And one night I got up to go, stood there for a full minute waiting for it to start, and got back in bed. Then got up again twenty minutes later.
My father spent the last decade of his life planning his whole world around the nearest men's room. Then the catheter. Then the surgery that didn't fully fix it.
The science was clear. Pumpkin seed oil was my answer. I was sure of it.
My wife Linda found a health blog with dosing recommendations. Two tablespoons of cold-pressed pumpkin seed oil, mixed into a morning smoothie. We ordered a bottle from Amazon. Thirty-eight dollars.
Every single morning. Seven days a week. I measured out two tablespoons, poured them into my blender with a banana and almond milk, and hit the button.
The oil never really blended in. No matter how long I ran it. There was always a slick film that separated and floated to the top. A greasy, grassy layer that coated the inside of the glass and clung to the back of my throat.
The taste was nutty at first. Then bitter. Then just overwhelming. By week three, the smell of it in the morning made my stomach turn before I even poured the glass.
Every. Single. Morning.
Linda started making the smoothie some mornings when I left early for work. She'd text me a photo of the glass waiting in the fridge. A little heart emoji.
We were in this together.
I started keeping a log. Nighttime trips. Flow, strong or weak. How long I stood there waiting.
Week 1: Still up three times a night. No change.
"Give it time," Linda said. "The studies said it takes a few weeks."
Week 2: Flow unchanged. Still dribbling.
Week 3: Still standing at the toilet waiting for it to start. Still up three times a night.
Week 4: If anything the urgency was worse.
I stood at the toilet at 3 AM. Waited. Waited. Gave up. Got back in bed.
I sat at the kitchen table. The empty smoothie glass still had an oily ring at the bottom. I pushed it away.
"It takes longer," Linda said from the doorway. She didn't sound convinced.
Week 6: No change.
Week 8: Flow still weak.
Week 11: Still up three times a night.
Eleven weeks. Seventy-seven consecutive mornings. Over thirty dollars a week between the oil, the bananas, and the almond milk. A blender with a greasy film no amount of scrubbing could fully cut.
And I was still getting up three times a night. Every single night.
My next appointment was in ten weeks. My doctor had been clear at the last visit: "If your symptoms don't improve, we're going to talk about adding a medication. And Andre, depending on how the prostate looks, we may need to have a conversation about a procedure."
I knew what conversation he meant.
That night I didn't sleep. I lay in the dark and thought about my father.

And before you stop reading, I need to tell you why I started this company, because it wasn't to sell supplements. It was because I watched my father di…
Please don't buy Elivora Pumpkin Seed Oil softgels. We told you it would help your prostate. We didn't warn you what it feels like to sleep through the night again after three years of getting up four times.
I'm Christopher. Co-founder of Elivora. And before you stop reading, I need to tell you why I started this company, because it wasn't to sell supplements. It was because I watched my father disappear from his own life, one bathroom trip at a time, and every doctor told him it was normal.
My father did everything right. He saw the urologist every six months. He took the pill they gave him. He did the watch-and-wait they told him to do. He followed the plan.
He still got up three, four, five times a night. He'd stand at the toilet and wait for a stream that wouldn't come, and when it came it was weak, and it stopped and started, and he never felt empty. He quit drinking water after 4pm so the nights wouldn't be worse. He mapped every outing around where the bathrooms were. He stopped driving the two hours to see my sister because he couldn't make it without stopping twice.
I watched him shrink. Not just his sleep. Him.
He stopped fishing. He used to be on the water before sunrise. Now he sat in his chair, and I could see in his face that he'd decided this was just getting old. Watched. Managed. Slowly getting worse while everyone told him it was normal for a man his age.
I couldn't accept that.
I started reading. Not blogs. Not Facebook groups. Published clinical research. I spent seven months going through studies on natural compounds that affect the prostate. Beta-sitosterol. Saw palmetto. Pygeum. Stinging nettle. Zinc. I read hundreds of papers. Most of the evidence was thin, or the dose was too low to mean anything, or the compound was wrong for what my father needed.
Then I found pumpkin seed oil.
Not the pumpkin seeds you snack on. The oil pressed from them, cold, before heat can ruin it. The research showed the oil is rich in plant sterols. Beta-sitosterol and a group called delta-7 sterols. And once I understood what those sterols actually do, I couldn't unsee why every product had failed him.
The prostate swells because of a hormone. DHT. Your body makes it from testosterone, and the older a man gets, the more it collects in the prostate and the more the prostate grows. A bigger prostate squeezes the tube you urinate through. That's the weak stream. That's the getting up all night. That's the feeling that you're never empty.
Two things have to happen. You have to slow down how much DHT the body is making. And you have to stop the DHT that's already there from locking onto the prostate.
The sterols in the pumpkin seed oil handle the second part. But the oil alone only does half the job. So I paired it with saw palmetto, which works on the first part, the making of the DHT. One without the other is half a solution. Every product I found did one, at best. Most did neither and just sold you the oil.
So I bought every prostate supplement and every pumpkin seed oil I could find. Amazon. Health stores. The pharmacy. I gave them to my father and I tried them myself. Nothing moved. So I read the labels.
Most were made from oil that had been heat-processed, and heat destroys the fragile sterols that do the actual work, so the label promised something the bottle no longer contained. The ones that were cold-pressed were underdosed. 250, 300, 500mg. A fraction of what the studies used. And almost none of them included saw palmetto, so even the good oil was only doing half the job.
I realized the science was real but every product on the market was built wrong.
So I went to Styria.
Styria is a region in Austria that has grown a particular pumpkin for its oil for over a century. The seeds come out dark, almost black, and the oil pressed from them is among the richest in sterols in the world. I found a grower there producing it the old way. I watched the seeds get cold-pressed. No heat. No solvents. I had the oil tested for sterol

My father stopped buying pumpkin seed oil because none of it ever worked. So I flew to Styria to source the real thing. He was up 3-4 times a night. In 12 weeks that came down to 0-1, after three years of the wrong stuff never touching it.
I'm Christopher. Co-founder of Elivora. And before you scroll past, I need to tell you why this company exists, because it wasn't to sell supplements. It was because I watched my father get quietly worse for years while every product that promised to help him was built wrong.
He did everything right. Flomax every night. The blood pressure pill, the cholesterol pill, the baby aspirin. He ate what his doctor told him to eat. He walked every morning. He kept every appointment. He never missed a dose.
And he was still up four times a night. Still standing at the toilet waiting for a stream that took forever to start and stopped before he felt done. Still planning every drive and every store around where the bathroom was. His doctor kept saying "this is normal at your age, we're managing it."
I watched him shrink. Not just his health. Him.
He stopped going to the early fishing trips because he couldn't sit in a boat that long. He stopped his Saturday coffee because the drive was too far from a restroom. He spent more time in his chair, and I could see in his face that he had decided this was just how it goes now. Managed. Monitored. Getting slowly worse while everyone acts like the pills are enough.
Somewhere in there he started buying pumpkin seed oil.
I don't know who told him. A neighbor, something he read, one of those morning segments. But he started ordering it and he kept ordering it for three years.
The one with thousands of reviews on Amazon. The one from the health food store with the wooden shelves and the man who seemed to know things. The expensive practitioner brand his friend swore by. The organic one. The one that said clinically studied on the front.
I'd visit and there would be another bottle on the counter next to the pill organizer.
He took it every single day for three years.
He was still up four times a night.
The last time I saw him open a new bottle he said "I don't think this does anything." He wasn't angry. That was the part that got me. He had stopped expecting things to work.
That night I went home and started reading.
Not blogs. Not Facebook groups. Published research. Months of it, going through studies on the compounds that actually affect the prostate. Beta-sitosterol. Saw palmetto. Pumpkin seed oil. Hundreds of papers. A lot of the evidence was thin, or the mechanism was wrong for what he needed.
And then I hit the real prostate research and I could not make it fit.
Because the studies were not thin.
A trial published in The Lancet in 1995 put beta-sitosterol against a placebo in men with enlarged prostates. The men on beta-sitosterol saw their urine flow improve and their symptom scores drop. The placebo group barely moved.
Then the pumpkin seed oil studies. A trial measured men on pumpkin seed oil using the standard symptom score urologists use in the clinic, and the score came down over the course of the study. A separate European study, the one the old German formulas were built on, found pumpkin seed specifically reduced how many times men were getting up in the night. [VERIFY specific figures before production — Hong 2009, Vahlensieck 2015]
Here is what that actually means.
Testosterone isn't the problem. What the body does with it is. As a man gets older, more of his testosterone gets converted into a stronger hormone called DHT. DHT collects in the prostate. The prostate grows. And the prostate sits wrapped around the tube that carries urine out of the body, so as it grows, it squeezes that tube. That is the weak stream. That is the getting up at night. That is standing there feeling like you're never quite finished.
His Flomax relaxed the muscle around that tube for a few hours at a time. It loosened the squeeze. It never once touched the DHT that was mak
